From I there are paths to E–L as discussed. So 3 edges not enough.

However, if we choose (J,L) and cut a single edge that separates I and P and D collectively from reaching B/E/M? Is there a bottleneck edge from the right side into the left chain {B,N,Y,O}? The right side connects to the left chain mainly through C–B and H–B and C–N/Y via long edges? The picture shows:
- C connects to B (edge B–C).
- H connects to B (edge H–B).
- P connects to O (edge P–O). O connects along bottom to Y; Y–N–B. So right side reaches B via two disjoint routes: through C->B/H->B and through P->O->Y->N->B. Therefore at least two cuts needed there.

Given complexity, isolating L seems easier: remove (L,E) and (L,M). That alone isolates L except for direct (L,J). So cut set: (L,J), (L,E), (L,M) of size 3. Need to verify minimality: Could size 2 suffice? To disconnect with 2 edges, you would need to either remove (L,J) and some single edge that blocks all J-to-{E,M} paths. As argued, J can reach M via J–I–H–G–F–M and reach E via J–I–C–B–E or via P/O/Y/N/B/E. These are edge-disjoint after leaving J, so a single extra edge cannot block both E and M simultaneously. Therefore at least 3 edges are needed. The set {(L,J),(L,E),(L,M)} works and is minimal.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(L, J), (L, E), (L, M)]

- Paths starting with G–H are impossible.
- Paths starting with G–F are impossible, and G–K and G–X cannot reach N because their only continuation is to F which is cut off.

- If only (G, H) is removed, paths remain through G–F–… to N.
- If only (G, F) is removed, paths remain through G–H–… to N.

Hence the minimum cut has size 2.

Option C: Remove (H, I) and (P, I). Then path G–…–J–I is still available.

- C is reachable via G–H–B–C.

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning

- E connects to B, L, M, F, G.
- H connects to G, I, B.

All E–H paths must leave E via one of {B, L, M, F, G} and arrive at H via one of {G, I, B}.

Observe disjoint route options:
1) Through B: E–B–H is a direct 2-edge path. To break any “via B” route, we must remove either (E, B) or (B, H).
2) Through G: E–G–H is also a direct 2-edge path. To break any “via G” route, we must remove either (E, G) or (G, H).
3) Through I: Paths like E–(F/M/L/K/X…)–J–I–H or E–B–C–I–H exist. As long as H–I remains, there is a route bypassing B and G. So to destroy all “via I” routes, remove (H, I).

Minimal cut:
- Remove (H, I) to eliminate all paths that reach H from the right side (via C/J/P…), independent of B or G.
- Still remaining are E–B–H and E–G–H. We must cut one edge from each of these two independent 2-edge paths.
  - Cut (E, B) to kill all via-B paths.
  - Cut (E, G) to kill all via-G paths. (Alternatively, cutting (B, H) and (G, H) instead of the two at E also works, but still needs two edges in addition to (H, I).)

Verification:
- After removing {(H, I), (E, B), (E, G)}, there is no edge into H except from B or G, both severed from E. Any route from E to the right (through L/M/F/X/K to J/I/C/P/O/…) cannot reach H because (H, I) is removed. Therefore, all E–H paths are broken.
- No smaller cut works: at least one edge is needed to block the I-side, and at least one to block B-based routes, and at least one to block G-based routes. Thus minimum is 3.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(H, I), (E, B), (E, G)]

Therefore, to separate E from J, we must cut all connections into J’s neighborhood: remove the three edges (M, J), (I, J), and (P, J). After removing these:
- Any path using M is blocked (E–M–J and E–L–M–J).
- Any path using I is blocked (E–H–I–J).
- Any path using P is blocked (…–P–J), including routes through D since D connects only to P.
No other edge leads directly to J, so all E–J paths are eliminated.

Minimality check:
- If we remove only one or two of these edges, a remaining neighbor of J (among M, I, P) still provides a path from E to J via the corresponding subgraph. Hence at least three edges are required. Removing exactly these three disconnects them.

- Without B? Yes: …→C→N or …→O→N.
- Without O? Yes: …→C→N or …→B→N.
Hence no single node among {B, C, O} is a universal cut.

However, consider removing H and one of {I, P}. Would that disconnect M?
- If H is removed, the E-branch is cut. Remaining access from M is only via J.
- To block via J, removing either I or P is enough because:
  - If we remove I, any path J→… must use J–P. From P, to reach N you need P–O, but O then to N; that still works unless blocked elsewhere. But with I removed, there is still path J–P–O–N. So removing only I is not sufficient unless we also block O or C/B later.
- Instead, try removing H and O:
  - With H removed, M connects only via J.
  - From J, possible routes toward N must end at N via B or C or O. If O is removed, can J reach B or C to then reach N?
    - J→I→C→N uses C (still available): still reaches N.
    - J→I→C→B→N uses B: still reaches N.
  So H+O not sufficient.

Find a 2-node separator closer to N:
- Because N is adjacent to three nodes {B, C, O}, any cut that avoids removing two of these must sever all access to at least two of them from M side and the third from the other side—complex.
- Minimal cut likely size 2 by removing two of {B, C, O}. Check if M can still reach the third without using removed ones, and then to N? It would need the N-edge to that third, which would exist. Therefore, to disconnect N, we must remove ALL neighbors of N that are reachable from M. Are any of B, C, O unreachable from M? From M through J and E paths, it seems all three are reachable:
  - M–J–I–C (reachable).
  - M–J–P–O (reachable).
  - M–E–H–B (reachable).
Thus, to separate M from N, we must remove all three neighbors {B, C, O} or block earlier branches appropriately.

Try a 2-node cut earlier that blocks all three channels:
- Node H feeds B and connects to I as well. If H is removed, paths to B from E are gone, but J-branch can still reach B via I→C→B. So H alone not enough.
- Node I is a major hub for J-branch and also receives from H. If we remove I, can M still reach N?
  - E-branch: M–E–H→B directly to N exists. So still connected via B.
- Node P removal leaves I path; still connected.
- What about removing C and O (two of N’s neighbors)? Then the only remaining neighbor is B. Can M reach B without using C or O? Yes: M–E–H–B or M–J–I–C–B but C removed; yet the E–H–B path remains. So we must remove also H or B. Hence size ≥3?

Check 3-node cut: {B, C, O} would trivially disconnect as N then only connects to Y, which is a leaf. Is there a 2-node cut anywhere? Consider removing H and J simultaneously:
- If J removed, M only connects via E. With H removed, E-branch also cut. But M still has neighbor E and L only; with H gone, E has no path to N? E connects to H only toward N; yes, E would be dead end. With J removed, no other route. Therefore {H, J} disconnects in 2 nodes.
- Verify minimality: Could 1 node disconnect? Removing J alone leaves M–E–H–… to N; removing H alone leaves M–J–… to N; removing E alone leaves M–J–…; removing I/P/B/C/O etc likely still leaves another route. So no single node works.

Hence minimal cut size is 2, with example {H, J}. Also alternatives: {H, I} does not cut because J–P–O–N exists. Wait if H and I removed, from M: via E blocked at H; via J need J–P–O–N, which connects, so not disconnected. So the pair must include J and either H or E.
- {E, J}: Removing J cuts J-branch; removing E cuts E-branch. Works.
- {H, J}: as argued, works.
- {J, B} also? If J and B removed, E–H–B path is blocked at B, but E–H–I–C–N still exists through H–I edge? There is an edge H–I yes. So with B removed, path M–E–H–I–C–N remains, so {J, B} not enough. {J, C}? M–E–H–B–N remains. {J, O}? M–E–H–B–N remains. So the cut must sever the E-branch at E or H plus sever J itself.

Conclusion:
Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [J, H] or [J, E]

Final Answer:
Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [J, H]

- Target nodes are B (left) and M (right).
- Neighbors of M: E and J.
- Therefore every B→M path must reach either E or J just before M.

Explore connections from B toward E and J:
- From B to E: B connects to H and C; E connects to F, L, M, and C (by the slanted edge B–E? Check: there is an edge from E down to C, not directly to B). Paths from B to E include:
  1) B–H–(… via G–F)–E
  2) B–H–I–E (since I connects to E via a slanted edge? No, I connects to E via I–E? The visible edge is E–I through the H–I line intersection; yes E–I is present.)
  3) B–C–E (since C–E exists)
  4) B–C–O–P–I–E, etc. Many alternatives funnel to E via nodes {H, C, I, F}.
- From B to J: Paths like B–H–I–J, B–C–P–J, B–C–I–J, etc.

Key observation: To reach M, any path must pass through either E or J. Thus, a cut that removes both E and J will certainly disconnect M from B. Can we do it with a single node?
- If we remove only E, there remains B–H–I–J–M or B–C–P–J–M. So not enough.
- If we remove only J, there remains B–C–E–M or B–H–…–E–M. So not enough.

Is there any way to block all routes to both E and J using a single earlier node?
- Consider removing I: Paths via J could still be B–C–P–J–M (P–J exists), avoiding I. Paths via E could be B–C–E or B–H–G–F–E, avoiding I. So I alone is insufficient.
- Remove C: Paths via E still exist through B–H–G–F–E or B–H–I–E. Paths via J exist through B–H–I–J. Not sufficient.
- Remove H: Paths via E remain B–C–E; via J remain B–C–I–J or B–C–P–J. Not sufficient.
- Remove F or G or P, etc.: there are alternative routes to E and J via other nodes. No single node upstream intercepts both branches.

Therefore the minimum cut requires at least two nodes, and {E, J} is sufficient.

Verification:
- Removing E eliminates all B→…→E→M paths.
- Removing J eliminates all B→…→J→M paths.
- With both removed, M has no remaining neighbor other than the removed nodes, thus no path from B to M exists.
- No single-node removal can disconnect them, as shown by alternatives above.

Final Answer:
Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [E, J]

Thus any pair among {B,C,I,G} fails due to at least one direct edge from N to B or C leading to H through that same node. Hence we must include both B and C in the cut, or remove H’s other entrances plus block N’s access, but we cannot remove H or N.

Consider 3-node removals:
- If we remove B and C, we also must block I or the path N–O–P–I–H. N connects to O directly and to C (but C removed) and to Y; O connects to P and to C (and to Y base). What if the third node is P: {B,C,P}. Check remaining routes:
